5. *Passes when:* the link's `up over the <carrier> carrier` line and a `subbed` line for each of the six subscriptions are in the log, each at most 1,000 ms after `restoredAt`; each stream's line reads `replay` and asks from the cursor it held; B's `mawsd.log` has the matching `sub` lines, and B's `host.log` has a `session.events … from the journal` line for each stream after `restoredAt`, which is the proof that the cursors were evicted; each pane shows each marker of its session's last unseen turn once; the log has no `dropped a repeated event` line. Since step 2, on the direct launch `aDirect` saw exactly one upgrade request and `aHub`, still stalled, none. On the relay launch `aHub` saw exactly one request for `/api/machines/<ref>/socket`, and the log has no `up over the direct carrier` line: the direct lane's dials reached the held pipe and went unanswered, and the relay did not wait behind them. Probes, the connections that sent nothing, are not counted.
6. The hub's pipe is restored where it was not, still inside the window (on the relay launch it was restored at step 4, and that instant is the hub's restore), and the feed comes back: since step 2, `aHub` saw exactly one request for `/api/registry/socket?app=1`, and `[org-feed] connected` and `[org-feed] listings: last page` are each in the log at most 2,000 ms after the hub's restore.

Under `MAWS_REMOTE_LATENCY_PERF=1` step 5's 1,000 ms and step 6's 2,000 ms are held, and the run is void by the file's own load rule, never averaged. Under `MAWS_REMOTE_LATENCY_CHECK=1` the same lines and counts must be there, every time is recorded and none is held. The numbers go to `test-results/remote-wake.json`, with each stream's `J` (the milliseconds of B's `sub` line), the rows its journal read took (B's `host.log` line) and the time of its first event after `subbed` (the link's line) beside its time. The first test and its bounds are untouched. The case adds about four to five minutes to the group, and its two settles (Decision Log 33 (b)) up to six more: at least 30 s each, since an admission takes two reads 30 s apart.

What the case shows of §F's budget: the lanes, the counts, and `J` for two evicted cursors on journals of about 6,500 events (the first launch) and 13,000 (the second), with every round trip near zero. `J` is a few tens of ms there, and the 1 s is held for that and claims nothing of a longer history. What it does not show: `J` on a long journal, a catch-up, a path that returns after the floor (row 12: the restore here comes 1 to 3 s after the wake), and the hub stalled above TCP while direct works; the last two are `link.test.ts`'s cases; the hub's pipe is not put on hold here because the feed, whose own bound this case holds, opens through it.

The project socket is not in the case. The scene's app runs the development session (`MAWS_DEV_ORG`), and the project channels are composed in the production session alone (`createChannels` in `src/main/org/org-session.ts`). Its one open after a wake is pinned by §G's unit tests and read live in 7e, where a Channel tab is left open.

### §J. The owner on a peer message

sminos writes the sender session's owners into its frame line, between the sender's alias and the arrow or the id (`compose_chat_frame`, `compose_frame` and `sender_labels` in doperpowers' `sminos/skills/sminos/scripts/sminos.py`, pinned there by `sminos/tests/test_words.py`):

    [sminos chat <project> <writer>:<seq> from <alias>, owned by <name>[, <name>…] → <target>[ | unread N]]
    [sminos message from <alias>, owned by <name>[, <name>…] id=<msg_id>]

A `<name>` is an owner's email, or its member id where the endpoint had no email. In place of `, owned by …` the label may be ` (owners unverified)`, ` (unverified, legacy)`, ` (signed by a revoked key)` or ` (unverified)`, or absent. This repository's fixtures hold no labelled frame today (`src/shared/engine/peer-envelope.test.ts`, the lane A tests): M2b adds them, in sminos's own words from that test.

*The alias has two forms, and the parse reads both.* Today sminos writes the alias exactly as its writer wrote it. The desk track will change sminos, after its M3, to write the alias as a JSON string literal whenever it holds any of `,` `(` `)` `"` `→` `[` `]` or a newline, and bare otherwise, so that the label is always outside the alias.

    // src/shared/engine/peer-envelope.ts
    /** The owners a sminos frame line attests for its sender; null for a frame with any other label or none. */
    export function sminosFrameOwners(frame: string): { alias: string; owners: string[] } | null;
    // src/renderer/src/transcript/model/rows.ts: PeerMessage.sender gains
    owners: string[] | null;   // the frame's names as written; null: the frame attested none
    // src/renderer/src/sidebar/owners.ts
    /** A frame's names as member ids: an email that `members` holds is its member's id; any other name stays as written. */
    export function ownerIdsOf(names: readonly string[], members: ReadonlyMap<string, OrgMember>): string[];
    // ownerEntries and ownersLabel gain a last parameter: unknown?: (id: string) => string, FORMER_MEMBER by default

- *The parse.* The sender text is what stands between `from ` and the arrow of a chat frame, or the ` id=` of a message frame. Where it opens with `"`, the alias is the JSON string literal at its start, decoded as one, and the search for a chat frame's arrow starts after that literal, so a quoted alias may hold ` → ` or `]` (`chatFrameSender` learns this; a literal that does not parse makes the line no frame). Otherwise the alias runs to the first `,` or ` (`, or to the end. What follows the alias is the label. `sminosFrameOwners` answers only for a label that is `, owned by ` and then names split on `, `, each one token with no space, comma or parenthesis; for any other label, or none, it answers null. What `sminosFrameSender` returns stays the sender text as written, so lane A's reading of today's frames is unchanged; how lane A draws a quoted alias is the desk track's when sminos ships it.
- *The model.* `derive.ts` already parses each `user.message` of peer origin to decide that its companion event draws it. It keeps, by `messageUuid`, the owners of an envelope whose `frame` is not null, and `inboundRow` puts them on the row of the `peer.message` with the same `messageUuid`. An event with no `messageUuid`, an outbound row and an in-process agent's message have `owners: null`.
- *The drawing.* In `PeerMessageRow` of `src/renderer/src/transcript/view/rows/AgentMessage.tsx`, after the sender's `@ name`, a span marked `data-owners` in the secondary style the sidebar's label uses (`RowTitle`), holding `ownersLabel(ownerIdsOf(owners, members), me, members, (id) => id)`, with `me` and `members` from the sessions store as `useOwnersLabel` reads them. The rule is therefore Decision Log 111's, by the sidebar's own function: nothing when you alone own the sender's session, else "you" first, two names shown and "+N" past them. A name `members` does not hold, as when signed out, is drawn as written. The span's `title` lists every owner.
- *What the label is worth.* It is a display of sminos's frame, not a verification by MAWS: MAWS checks no signature here and draws what the line says. With today's sminos the alias is unconstrained, so an alias that holds `, owned by x` reads as a label on an event sminos left unlabelled, and one that holds a comma before a real label voids it. Until sminos writes such an alias as a string literal, the owner MAWS draws on a peer message is as trustworthy as sminos's frame and no more. M5 writes the tracker row, with the desk track as the fix's owner and the sminos version recorded when it ships.

Unit: `peer-envelope.test.ts` (both frame kinds with one owner and with two, an owner by id; each other label and no label answer null; a name with a space answers null; a quoted alias that holds `, owned by x`, ` → @you` and `]` before a real label yields the decoded alias and the real owners; a quoted alias with no label answers null; a bare alias that holds `, owned by x` and no label reads as owned by x, which is today's limit and is named so in the test); `derive` (a peer message whose envelope carries a labelled frame has `sender.owners`, one without has null, pairing by `messageUuid`); `peer.test.tsx` (no span when the one owner is you; "you, Ana" when you share it; another member's name when you do not own it; a name unknown to `members` as written).

### §K. The 7e probe and the evidence lines (`test/support/wake-probe.mjs`)

One Node script with no dependency, so that it can be copied to a machine and run with `node` alone. It lives in `test/support/`, beside the suites' other standalone script (`latency-engine-stub.mjs`): `scripts/` is M1's ground, and a file here is covered by the unit suite's `test/**/*.test.ts`.

    node test/support/wake-probe.mjs watch --target <name>=<host>:<port> [--target …] [--https <url>] --out <dir>
    node test/support/wake-probe.mjs read <dir> --after <time> [--at <time> …]
    node test/support/wake-probe.mjs extract <log file> [--since <time>]

- `watch` is the app's own probe, on purpose. For each target a bare TCP connect starts every 100 ms; each is given 3 s; they overlap; one that connects is closed at once. Each try is one JSON line in `<dir>/<name>.jsonl`, written when it ends: its number, `started` and `ended` on the wall clock in ms, `ms`, its duration on Node's monotonic clock, `ok`, and the error's code when it failed. With `--https` it also makes one GET of that URL every 2 s, each given 3 s, and writes each one's start, end and status or error to `<dir>/https.jsonl`: this is Decision Log 6 (k)'s cross-check, a request that succeeds while the probes to the same host fail. It runs until it is interrupted.
- *The clock.* A try's `started` and `ended` are read from the wall clock at those moments, because `main.log`'s times are the wall clock's and the comparison is between the two files. One monotonic clock tied to the wall clock once at the start would be wrong after the sleep if Node's monotonic clock does not advance through it, and this repository does not show whether it does (§D). The monotonic clock gives only a try's own duration, and no try that counts spans the sleep.
- `read` counts only tries that started after `--after`, which is the time of `main.log`'s `[wake]` line. A try that started before it, one that spans the sleep among them, is listed as excluded and counts for nothing: not for reachability, not for a median. For each target `read` takes, of the counted tries that succeeded, the one that started first. `reachable` is that try's `started`, the earliest moment the path can have been reachable for it, so the try's own duration is counted against the pass and not for it. It prints that try's `started`, `ended` and `ms`, and for each `--at` time given (the time of a log line) the time from the try's start and from its end. It also prints the median `ms` of the counted successful tries in the 30 s from `reachable`: that is `h` for the hub's target and `d` for a machine's direct port.
- `m` is read on the machine: the script is copied there and `watch --target hub=<the hub's host>:443` is run for 30 s after the wake, and read with `--after` the moment it began; its median is the machine's own round trip to the hub.
- `extract` prints the lines of a `main.log`, a `mawsd.log` or a `host.log` whose text, after the time, matches one of the patterns below, and prints nothing else. It is the only way a line leaves a machine for the evidence directory (§H).

The evidence patterns. Each is matched against the whole text after a line's time; `<n>` is digits, `<id>`, `<ref>`, `<key>`, `<host>`, `<position>` and `<cursor>` are runs with no space, and `…` is any text to the end of the line or to the next literal:

    [wake] slept <n> ms (resume|clock)
    [reach] window open for 60 s (wake|network)
    [reach] <host>:<n>: reachable after <n> probes, <n> ms
    [reach] <host>:<n>: window closed after <n> probes, none connected (…)
    [machines] <ref>: wake after <n> ms: dropped …
    [machines] <ref>: probe <word>: …
    [machines] <ref>: attempt (direct|relay|today's): catch-up, dial <n>
    [machines] <ref>: attempt failed (direct|relay|today's): …
    [machines] <ref>: lane (direct|relay): spent (…)
    [machines] <ref>: floor after <n> ms: today's attempts from here (…)
    [machines] <ref>: lanes again: <host>:<n> answered, <n> ms after the floor
    [machines] <ref>: wrote <n> act message(s), <n> bytes, ahead of the subscriptions
    [machines] <ref>: up over the (direct|relay) carrier…
    [machines] <ref>: the … channel was lost (…); reconnecting
    [machines] <ref>: subscription <id> …: subbed (replay|snapshot|missing) at <position>, asked from <cursor>
    [machines] <ref>: subscription <id> …: first (event|snapshot) after subbed, at <position>
    [machines] <ref>: subscription <id>: dropped a repeated event at <position>
    [org-feed] wake after <n> ms: …
    [org-feed] no pong within <n> ms: dropped the socket
    [org-feed] probe: …
    [org-feed] connected to <host>
    [org-feed] listings: last page, <n> records in <n> pages
    [org-channel] <8 hex>: wake after <n> ms: …
    [org-channel] <8 hex>: no pong within <n> ms: dropped the socket
    [org-channel] <8 hex>: welcomed, resuming after <n>
    [org-channel] <8 hex>: caught up at <n>
    endpoint: channel <key> sub <id> …: subbed (replay|snapshot|missing) at <position>, asked from <cursor>, after <n> ms
    endpoint: the hub connection …; connecting again in <n> ms
    ring evicted <id> launch <id>: events through seq <n> are the journal's now
    session.events <id> after (<n>|start): from the journal, <n> rows read in <n> ms[, from the checkpoint at seq <n>]
    checkpoint <id> launch <id> at seq <n>: <n> bytes; <n> held, <n> bytes

What `…` stands for in these lines is a carrier's name, a reason word, an error's words (§H) or a subscription's `<what>`: none is a session's text.

Unit (`test/support/wake-probe.test.ts`, which runs the script as a child process): `extract` over a file that holds one sample of every pattern mixed with a line that carries a file path and a line of free text prints exactly the samples; `read` over a recorded directory names, for each target, the counted try that started first among those that succeeded, the times from its start and from its end to each `--at`, and the median; a recording that holds a try started before the wake's time and ended after it lists that try as excluded and takes the next; `watch` against a loopback listener that refuses and then accepts records tries 100 ms apart that overlap, and a first success.

### §L. Checkpoints in the session stream: Task 8, built in M2b if the owner rules so (Decision Log 8); otherwise the body of a board ticket

*What it is for.* §F's `J` grows with a launch's history because a journal read for a cursor the ring has evicted starts at the journal's first row. A checkpoint lets it start near the cursor, so that a read costs at most one interval of rows whatever the launch's length.

*A checkpoint* is the journal's position between two rows with the normaliser's state at that point:

    // src/engine-host/session-stream.ts
    export const CHECKPOINT_ROWS = 10_000;              // a checkpoint after each row whose seq is a multiple of this
    export const CHECKPOINTS_DENSE = 24;                // the newest boundaries, every one kept
    export const CHECKPOINT_SPARSE_EVERY = 8;           // of the older boundaries, one in this many is kept
    export const CHECKPOINTS_MAX = 40;                  // for one stream
    export const CHECKPOINT_BYTES = 2 * 1024 * 1024;    // the states' buffers for one stream, in all
    interface Checkpoint {
      through: number;   // the seq of the last row the state has seen
      file: string;      // the journal file that holds that row, by name
      index: number;     // its place among the launch's files when the checkpoint was taken
      offset: number;    // bytes into it, just past that row
      state: Buffer;     // v8.serialize(normalizer.state), of the normaliser that has seen every row through `through`
    }
    // SessionStreamOptions gains checkpointRows?: number (tests); SessionStream gains checkpoint(c: Checkpoint): void
    // restored with v8.deserialize(state) and given to `new Normalizer({ …, state })`; nothing is added to normalizer.ts

The state is copied with `v8.serialize` from `node:v8`, the structured clone format, and restored with `v8.deserialize`; no encoding is written by hand. The copy is deep and shares nothing with the live normaliser. Maps, Sets, `NaN`, `-0` and `undefined` survive it, which a JSON text would not give: `Normalizer.streamEvent` keeps `Number(event.index)`, which is `NaN` for a frame with no index. Nothing in the JSON the state keeps from the wire (`outbound`'s requests, an `inbound` call's `args`) can collide with it. The buffer's `length` is the byte count the cap uses. The state holds nothing the format refuses: every field of `NormalizerState`, of a `Lane`, and of an `InboundEntry` with its `McpCall` (`src/engine-host/normalizer.ts`, `normalizer-controls.ts`) is a string, a number, a boolean, null, a plain object, an array, a Map or a Set of those, or JSON as the wire carried it; none is a function, a class instance or keyed by a symbol. The next row's seq is `through + 1`. A checkpoint is taken between two rows, so it carries no rows: `JournalCursor`'s `carried` is a page's remainder, not the journal's. Everything the normaliser keeps between rows is in `NormalizerState` (its other fields are its ids and its warning sink), and the constructor already takes a state.

*Where they are taken.* By the live path: when the session (`src/engine-host/session.ts`, which owns the journal's writer and the live normaliser) has appended and normalised a row whose seq is a multiple of `CHECKPOINT_ROWS`, it hands the stream a checkpoint; the writer shows its position for it (its current file, rotation index and bytes, of which the last two are private today). And by any journal scan: `fromJournal`, having pushed such a row through its normaliser, takes one where none is held for that row. So a stream adopted after an engine-host hand-over or a restart, which begins with none, rebuilds them by its first scan and goes on from the live path. They are kept in memory only: nothing new is written to disk, and a hand-over carries nothing of them.

*Which are kept.* With `head` the newest boundary held, a checkpoint at boundary `b` (its `through` over `CHECKPOINT_ROWS`) is kept while `head - b` is under `CHECKPOINTS_DENSE`, or `b` is a multiple of `CHECKPOINT_SPARSE_EVERY`. Then the oldest are dropped while more than `CHECKPOINTS_MAX` are held or their states' buffers pass `CHECKPOINT_BYTES`. So the newest 240,000 rows have a checkpoint every 10,000, which is more than four times the whole of the largest lane B journal on the MacBook today (about 55,000 rows); older history has one every 80,000; and a stream never holds more than 2 MiB of them. `dispose()` lets them go. Each one taken writes a `host.log` line: `checkpoint <appSessionId> launch <launchId> at seq <n>: <bytes> bytes; <count> held, <total> bytes`.

*How a read uses them.* `fromJournal`, when it cannot continue its kept page cursor, starts from the newest checkpoint whose `through` is at or before `afterSeq`, if the launch's files still hold that checkpoint's file at its `index` with every earlier file present, which is what today's scan needs of them: a fresh normaliser on the restored state, that file and offset, the next seq. With no such checkpoint (a read from the start, a cursor before the oldest kept, a file gone), or when the read from one fails, it is today's scan from the first row, with today's answer. The rows it reads are then at most `CHECKPOINT_ROWS` before the cursor plus the page's, for a cursor in the dense region; at most 80,000 plus the page's in the sparse one. §H's journal line says which: `…: from the journal, <rows> rows read in <ms> ms, from the checkpoint at seq <n>`.

*What grows with history.* A checkpoint's size is the state's, and parts of the state only grow: `completedBlocks` holds one entry for each API message the launch has seen and nothing removes one; `toolUseToAgent` and `safeguards` hold one for each tool use that launched an agent or carried a verdict; `taskTypes` and `workflowNames` one for each task and workflow; `subagentMawsUses`, `reseeded` and `reseededMessages` a few. At roughly 50 to 100 bytes an entry (an id of about 30 characters and a small value; an estimate from the shape, not a measurement), a launch of 1,000 messages makes each checkpoint on the order of 50 to 100 KiB and a launch of 10,000 on the order of 0.5 to 1 MiB. Under the 2 MiB cap such a long launch keeps only its newest two to four, and a cursor older than the oldest kept is read by today's scan: the bound holds fully for the launches the machines have today and weakens, to the cap's limit, for much longer ones. Removing entries the normaliser no longer needs would shrink every checkpoint, and is a change to the normaliser's own behaviour that is not proposed here.

*What the live path pays.* One `v8.serialize` of the state at each boundary, in the turn that normalised that row: tens of microseconds for a state of a few KiB and on the order of a millisecond or two for one of 0.5 MiB (an estimate from the state's shape; nothing was run). A session that streams 50 rows a second meets a boundary every 200 s. The latency bench's session writes under 1,000 rows and never meets one, so OX7's numbers cannot move (constraint 2).

*A launch whose journal may differ from what the live path saw* (Decision Log 30). A live checkpoint is taken only while every engine line of the launch has reached the journal as the live normaliser read it. `numbersReadBack` in `src/engine-host/journal.ts`, run by the session on each engine line's raw text before the row is journaled, is one string-aware pass: it jumps over each string literal and never looks inside one, and parses a number token only when it has an exponent, starts with `-0` or is longer than 300 characters, tripping when the token is a negative zero or overflows to an infinity. Every other number `JSON.stringify` writes back as the same double, so a digit run past a double's precision and an underflow to 0 do not trip it, and a unit case holds it to the journal's own round trip on 27 fixed tokens and 5,000 random ones. On the first line that trips it the launch takes no more live checkpoints, reads fall back to the checkpoints scans build, and `host.log` says so once: `<appSessionId>: live checkpoints off for launch <launchId> at seq <n>: an engine line holds a number the journal writes otherwise`. The exact-text comparison (Decision Log 30 (a)) was not taken: the fixtures' lines are rows the probe re-serialised itself (`spikes/protocol-probe/harness.mjs`), so their proof would be vacuous; it trips on escape-style differences inside strings; and the session does not hold the writer's line text. Its cost on the live path, measured over the fixtures' 21,514 engine lines (767 bytes on average, median of 30 rounds, under a load of about 20): 0.93 µs a line, against 2.06 µs for the `JSON.parse` the transport already pays on the same lines; 4 µs for the largest line, of 114,600 bytes.

*The idle bound* (P5 acceptance 14) does not move. Its measured scene keeps one session whose journal is far under 10,000 rows, so it holds no checkpoint and the gate's reading is what it was. On the owner's MacBook a Keep-marked idle session holds its stream's checkpoints for as long as it is kept: at most 2 MiB, and for a launch the size of today's largest about five of them, well under 1 MiB by the estimate above. M4's 14a and 14b read the checkpoint lines of the kept session and record their total beside the footprint.

*The wire and the answers do not change*: a read through a checkpoint returns the events today's scan returns, with their ids and seq. That is the proof's first part.

*Proof.* A property test in `session-stream.test.ts`: over journals whose rows are shaped from the probe fixtures, with a small `checkpointRows` and a small rotation size so that a journal has several boundaries and a rotation, and for random cursors, `snapshot(afterSeq, limit)` and the pages after it on a stream with checkpoints equal, event for event, the same calls on a stream with none. An assertion on rows read, not on time: for a cursor in the dense region at most one interval plus the page; the count is the one the `host.log` line carries. And: a checkpoint the live path took equals, byte for byte, the one a scan takes at the same row, where the live state holds no `undefined` member that the journal's JSON drops; where it does (a control request the host builds with one), restoring the live checkpoint and reading on from it gives, event for event, what a full scan gives (Decision Log 29). Today exactly one member of the state can hold such an `undefined`: an `outbound` entry's `request` (`src/engine-host/normalizer.ts`, where an `out` control request is kept), which holds by reference the object `ControlClient.exchange` builds (`src/engine-host/rpc.ts`, `{ subtype, ...payload }`) until its answer, and which the normaliser reads only by property. Every member kept from an `in` frame is from `JSON.parse` of an engine line, which is the same on both sides only for a line JSON carries through the journal unchanged (Decision Log 30: a `-0` or an overflowing literal is not), and every other member is derived through `typeof`, `?? null`, `String()` or `Number()`. A second case would come with a new member that keeps a raw subtree of an `out`, `meta` or `stderr` frame by reference, or a source of `in` frames that is not `JSON.parse`; the live-boundary test covers the one there is; the kept set after many boundaries is the rule's, and over the byte cap the oldest go; an adopted stream's first scan leaves checkpoints behind it; a checkpoint whose file is gone gives today's scan and today's answer. The property test's journals include stream events with no index, so that a `NaN` is in the state at a boundary, and payloads that hold objects shaped like an encoding's tag (`{"$map": …}`, `{"$set": …}`), which a read through a checkpoint must return as they were.

*Touches, if built.* `src/engine-host/session-stream.ts`, `src/engine-host/session.ts`, `src/engine-host/journal.ts`, `src/engine-host/ipc.ts`, and their tests. Then §I's case also asserts the rows read for its evicted cursors, and §F's bound is claimed whatever the launch's length inside the dense region. (§I's case asserts the bound on the rows each resume read took, one interval plus the page; its held cursors sit below the first boundary, so the case shows the bound holding and not the checkpoints in use, which the property test proves; Decision Log 29.)

### Dependencies

No new package. The repair page uses the renderer's existing build (React, Tailwind, the tokens). M2b's probes are `node:net`'s, and its pings and `terminate` the `ws` client's own. CI-opt's two pull requests on `.github/` (M1's gate lists). The desk track's registry M1 and M2 may land before F and are part of it.

## Surprises & Discoveries

- Observation: the embedded shape was not only a setting. A launch on the main profile whose LaunchAgent is absent, not loaded, refused or silent ran embedded without anyone choosing it (`planShape`, `prepareShape` in `src/main/hosts/daemon.ts`).
  Evidence: the inventory of the embedded root, §1.3, read against the code on 2026-11-10.
- Observation: both of a native Mac's signing keys, the channel key and the step-up key that is now production's root, are plaintext private JWKs in one file; Touch ID is a step in the app's flow and binds nothing to the key.
  Evidence: `src/main/device/keys.ts` (the record and its write), `src/main/device/step-up.ts`; the inventory's §5.
- Observation: a client settings file of a version newer than a build knows is read as the defaults, so a version bump here would cost a rolled-back machine its settings.
  Evidence: `migrateSettings` in `src/shared/settings/defaults.ts`: any other version throws, "which a store turns into the defaults with a warning".
- Observation: `e2e/c12-quit.spec.ts`'s embedded branch had not run since O3-B: the launcher defaults to the daemon shape and `e2e:c12` sets no mode. `e2e/artifacts-store.spec.ts`'s header said every case ran in utility mode; only the perf case did.
  Evidence: M1's executor, reading `e2e/support/launch.ts` and the scripts at 0fc844444.
- Observation: on `mawsd` the engine host's park exits as soon as its last session goes idle, so an app relaunched to read an idle release races the host's exit. The ported case holds a second session's turn open; an open turn is never released and keeps the host and `mawsd` up.
  Evidence: `e2e/supervisor.spec.ts` (e) at 27740743a; the base's embedded half relaunched before the supervisor's own idle exit.
- Observation: `src/services/spine-root.test.ts` belongs to vitest's `node-timing` project, not `node`; a focused run names that project.
  Evidence: M1's focused runs.
- Observation: the org feed's wake probe returned at once whenever a socket object existed, and the feed never wrote on its socket and had no liveness check, so a feed socket that died silently during a sleep stayed dead until the session's next change or a relaunch.
  Evidence: `probe()` in `src/main/org/registry-feed.ts` at master e8be3867e (`if (closed || socket !== null || parked) return;`); the readiness read of 2026-11-10.
- Observation: the direct listener charges its per-source bucket at the upgrade request, before the WebSocket exists, so anything that opens WebSockets to learn whether a path is back spends the tokens the handshake needs.
  Evidence: `#upgrade` in `src/endpoint/direct-listener.ts` (`this.#limiter.take(source)` before `handleUpgrade`); the desk track's read of 2026-11-10.
- Observation: neither hub object completes a close handshake, so a client `close(1000)` on the feed or on a project socket ends as 1006 about 10 s later; and the hub's runtime answers a protocol ping without waking the object.
  Evidence: the desk track's staging run of 2026-11-10 (Decision Log 6, (d)).
- Observation: sminos's frame line carries the sender session's owners, and no fixture of this repository held a labelled frame, so the label's form had to be read from sminos's own source and test.
  Evidence: doperpowers' `sminos/skills/sminos/scripts/sminos.py` (`sender_labels`) and `sminos/tests/test_words.py`; `src/shared/engine/peer-envelope.test.ts`, whose frames carry no label.
- Observation: a handshake is not always one dial. `connectChannel` dials again after a `behind` that advanced a head, up to 16 connections, and on the direct carrier each redial is another upgrade and another token. So "one upgrade and one handshake" is the common case, not a property.
  Evidence: `connectChannel` and `MAX_CATCH_UP_CONNECTIONS` in `apps/org-protocol/src/channel.ts`; the `dial` function of `viaDirect` in `src/main/machines/remote/link.ts`; the adversarial review of d2353ab77 (Decision Log 8).
- Observation: the endpoint answers a stream's `sub` only after several reads, and for a cursor the ring has evicted one of them reads the launch's journal from its first row. Round trips do not bound that work.
  Evidence: `SessionStreams.#open` in `src/shared/host/stream-catchup.ts` (the status, the `session.events` read that checks the cursor, then the queue, the pending items and the catalog, all before `subbed`); `SessionStream.snapshot` and `fromJournal` in `src/engine-host/session-stream.ts` ("Any other `afterSeq` starts over from the first row"); `RING_SIZE` 5,000.
- Observation: a second consumer of the direct bucket cannot be made safe by a check alone. `viaDirect` reads the bucket before it awaits the open and charges it after, which is sound only because one attempt runs at a time; the listener charges when the upgrade arrives. Running today's attempt beside the lanes would have put two diallers on one balance that reserves nothing.
  Evidence: `viaDirect` in `src/main/machines/remote/link.ts` (`bucket.holds()` before `raceDirect`, `bucket.take()` after; its own comment, "One attempt runs at a time, so the token `holds` saw is still there when the socket opens"); round 2 of the adversarial review (Decision Log 9).
- Observation: the journal read for an evicted cursor costs about 35 ms for each 10,000 rows before the cursor, so the endpoint's time to `subbed` grows with a launch's history: 157 ms at 50,000 rows, 670 ms at 200,000, 1,714 ms at 500,000, against 0.07 ms for a cursor the ring holds.
  Evidence: the architect's bench of 2026-11-10 on master caaef871c, `timings.tsv` under `~/.local/share/maws-architect/o15-evidence/resume-cost/` (the `justEvicted` rows: 15.6, 157.2, 670.5 and 1,713.6 ms median for the whole opening).
- Observation: a lane A stream is answered `subbed` before anything is read, and after any sleep over two minutes its answer is a snapshot under a fresh upstream, whose content is sminos's to send.
  Evidence: `PlaneA.open` and `#fresh` in `src/endpoint/plane-a.ts` (`sub.subbed('snapshot', …)` in the turn the `subscribe` is sent); `close`'s timer of `RESUME_MS`, 120 s.
- Observation: the log writer the hosts share creates its directory outside its guard, leaves an existing directory's mode as it is and counts the file's size privately, so two processes on one file can rotate each other's log away.
  Evidence: `openSupervisorLog` in `src/engine-host/supervisor-log.ts` (`mkdirSync` before the `try`; `size` read once); the adversarial review of d2353ab77.
- Observation: the hub's own work on a WebSocket upgrade is about 220 to 240 ms, some 190 ms of it the Access check and the Registry round trip that every request pays. It is the largest single share of the relay carrier's second after a wake.
  Evidence: the desk track's staging measurement of 2026-11-10 (Decision Log 6, (j)): medians of 252 ms for the feed and 255 ms for the Relay's app side from TLS done to the 101, over a network round trip of 15 to 35 ms.
- Observation: the artifacts store's keystroke-to-durable-ack p95 on the utility shape drifted from 7.4 ms (2026-10-15) and 8.3 ms (2026-10-19) to 9.5 ms (2026-11-10), so the 10 ms bound had half a millisecond left before M1 moved the case to `mawsd`, whose relay hop costs about 1.1 ms at p95.
  Evidence: run 42839107435 on master a2624903f beside run 42837591610 on c401a8f1e, the same machine within 15 minutes; the October records under `docs/doperpowers/evidence/p3-finish/measurements/`; Decision Log 13 (c).
- Observation: on master, a click on a session row with ⌘ or the middle button opens it in place like a plain click, and a remote session's connect replaces the active tab, so "open side by side" in the wake case's scene left one tab.
  Evidence: `ListedRow.tsx` (the click handler reads no modifier) and `remote-connect.ts` (the tab replaced); the shakedown 42856948906 on 9aada1e8d failing at `openSideBySide`; the case now pins the first tab (998da50f2).
- Observation: on master, a live session stream and a replay of its journal already disagree for an engine frame whose numbers JSON cannot carry: a literal `1e400` is `Infinity` live and `null` in the journal, a `-0` is `-0` live and `0` in the journal.
  Evidence: Task 8's review's repro (Decision Log 30); the detector `numbersReadBack` in `src/engine-host/journal.ts` keeps checkpoints out of such a launch, the disagreement itself is outside M2b.
- Observation: `--gates all` runs the packaged jobs (`packaged 1/2`, `2/2`), so a separate `--gates packaged` on the same tree repeats them.
  Evidence: run 42870615035 on 4b8f99326.
- Observation: the early measured run of the wake case on 4b8f99326 was void by Rule B (peak 6.71, 30 s pair 4.92) on a held mini with nothing else running: the case's own work raised the 1-minute load from 4.13 at its start to 6.71 at its end (samples 4.13, 4.23, 4.66, 4.76, 4.23, 6.65). The code found no failure and no bound miss at those loads: direct, the link up 54 ms and the last `subbed` 154 ms after the restore, the feed connected 47 ms and its last page 48 ms after the hub's; relay, 76, 769, 66 and 67 ms; one upgrade and one feed open on each. The streams' `J` was 38 and 46 ms on the direct launch and 437 and 439 ms on the relay's, whose journal reads were 7,578 and 7,568 rows in 149 and 152 ms from the first row (cursors near seq 7,057, before the first checkpoint at 10,000; bounds 10,521 and 10,515). Not a verdict.
  Evidence: run 42906030773, its `remote-wake.json` (artifact `measured-e2e-remote-latency`); Decision Log 33.
- Observation: the wake case's stall is not short. It lasts as long as B's writing of 21 turns to each session, about 45 s, past the link's silence check (three periods of 5 to 7 s), so both carriers' links met the inject `down/none` and had been retrying for some 42 s; the scene models a wake after a loss the link already noticed, not one in which the app believes its channel is up.
  Evidence: `linkBeforeWake` `down/none` and a `lost (timeout)` line on both carriers, and `attempt failed (today's)` lines at −45,146 and −46,934 ms from the restore, in run 42906030773's `remote-wake.json`; Decision Log 33 (a).

## Decision Log

1. **2026-11-10, the architect, at authoring: the verification.** This document's M1 and M2 are reviewed for buildability by `doperpowers:adversarial-reviewer` before the executor starts. The executor's loop reviews each milestone at its boundary. The branch is reviewed at `doperpowers:reviewer-high` before it lands, for two things above all: what a launch on the owner's main profile does when the daemon cannot start, and that no path leaves the machine's data with two writers or none. Its gates are `--gates all` and `--gates packaged` on the head, with the measured groups M1 ported. M3 to M5 are verified by their records: every result in M4's table names its evidence, and a result with none is not a pass.
2. **2026-11-10, the architect, at the adversarial review of M1 and M2 (three findings, all taken).** (a) The first draft's launch table let a profile with a private daemon succeed at once while acceptance 2 promised the window for any daemon that cannot start. The contract is narrowed, not the code widened: the window is the main profile's launch outcome, and a private daemon that fails to start, or any daemon that fails after the launch, shows where it does today (acceptance 2; M2, "What the window does not cover"). The alternative, a start and a welcome awaited for every private daemon before the app composes, was not taken: it changes the start of every launch the suites make, for a failure only a broken build produces. Because §C's fault then proves the window and not the unit's rows, the rows stay on unit tests over a faked runner and M4 gains a live check of the window on the MacBook. (b) Acceptance 3's proof moved from a hand-built profile, which the library's first reconcile would empty, to the cross-build scene whose peer really runs embedded; §C's fifth case keeps only the saved setting. (c) M1 names two more tests of the static job that read the real workflow and `package.json` (`e2e-default-specs.test.mjs`, `ci-gate-alerts.test.mjs`), with the rule that nothing under `.github/` or `scripts/` names a deleted script or spec. The reviewer's second reading, of these changes alone, found nothing material.
3. **2026-11-10, the owner, on the closing run (three questions through sminos).** An agent drives the live checks on the MacBook and the owner gives Touch ID ("Agent drives, I give Touch ID"); one 45-minute quiet window for item 14's reading and the real-network bench ("Yes, one 45-minute window"); the full hour asleep for item 7 ("Yes, the full hour"). So no claim of items 4, 7 or 14 starts as a ruling.
4. **2026-11-10, the executor, at pre-flight: the branch's base and its integration.** The architect's dispatch puts the branch on `origin/o15-doc` at 8e7fb3c40 (this document and its inventories, landing on master in parallel), not on `origin/master` as Concrete Steps say, and integrates master by merge, never by rebase. So M1's "Rebase onto master before the branch's gates" reads as: merge `origin/master` into the branch once the architect says `o15-doc` has landed, keeping both sides' edits of CI-opt's `.github/` changes. A merge keeps every commit this document and the report cite. M1's own proof (`--gates all` on M1's head, and the measured groups it ported) runs before M2 is built on it only if the architect clears the Mac for it then; otherwise the same runs on the branch's head carry both milestones, and the report says which.
5. **2026-11-10, the architect, answering the executor's checkpoint: no local typecheck, and the Mac at M1's boundary.** (a) The first draft allowed `pnpm typecheck` on the MacBook (constraint 6, Concrete Steps' "Locally" lines) against the owner's standing rule of 2026-11-02 that bars local typechecks on this Mac, a bigger heap included; the executor met it as a heap exhaustion in `tsc -p tsconfig.node.json`. A second correction the same day, from the rule as first recorded: no typecheck of any kind and no multi-file eslint or vitest at any load. Both places now allow a focused run alone, eslint on one file or vitest on one test file, with the typecheck, the whole lint and the unit suite in class 1a, and class 1a green on a head before any run that holds the CI Mac. (b) M1's proof is spent at M1's boundary, before M2 builds on it: class 1a green on M1's head, then `--gates all`, the measured groups M1 ported and `e2e:cross-build --peer 63443a3bd`, each cleared by the architect, since a fault found only after M2 could not be told apart from the deletion. On the branch's head after M2: `--gates all`, `--gates packaged` and `e2e:cross-build` again; the measured groups a second time only if M2 changed one of their specs or the code they measure in a way the executor cannot rule out, since M3 measures the landed build anyway. This settles Decision Log 4's conditional.
6. **2026-11-10, the architect, at the readiness read before F: what the read found is fixed before F is fixed (M2b), and F comes about a day later.** A research agent read the code at master e2ade600c, whose app code equals cf30dafdd, against M4's table; the architect checked its main citations, and every claim this amendment makes of the code was read in the code at master e8be3867e. (a) *The findings.* 7e could neither pass by design nor be observed: nothing reacted to the network's return; a link retried on 1, 2, 4 and 5 s and then every 30 s; an attempt could hang about 23 s; a dead relay socket was reused; the endpoint record was fetched before every dial; the org feed's wake probe did nothing while a socket object existed, and the feed had no liveness check; the project socket had no wake trigger; main's lines had no time and no file. 2c's record had nothing to say whether a replay came from the ring or the journal. `artifact_export` told the model it needs the app. 11c's owner was not drawn. `mawsd`'s failed hub connection logged an empty reason. The repair window's live check could meet a refused start that §A had no row for. (b) *The decision.* This document's rule for a claim that cannot pass is a fix that moves F, or the owner's ruling. The architect fixes: M2b, on a branch of its own beside M1 and M2, landing before M3. The cost is F about a day later and one more branch's gates on the CI Mac. The alternative, the asleep hour run on F as it stood and the miss put to the owner, would spend the owner's hour to confirm what the code already shows and leave a fix that moves F afterwards. (c) *The window's mechanism changed once.* The architect first decided staggered WebSocket opens with the handshake on the first to connect. The desk track then read the endpoint and the hub at master e8be3867e: the direct listener charges its bucket at every upgrade request; a new channel from a device is kept beside its old one, never in its place; the hub limits and replaces nothing but pays an Access check and a registry read for each open, and the Registry sends a whole snapshot at each feed open. So the thing tried often became a bare TCP connect, the upgrade and the handshake run once (§E, §F), and the feed and the project sockets reopen with one open in flight (§G). (d) *The ping.* 2026-11-10, the desk track, on staging (the hub at e181a194, from the MacBook with Node's `ws`): the Registry feed and one project socket, each idle 70, 140 and 210 s since its last frame, then a protocol ping; pongs in 19, 64 and 11 ms on the feed and 14, 9 and 8 ms on the project socket; every ping answered; no frame beside any pong; `wrangler tail` showed no event at either object during the rounds, so the runtime answers the ping itself without waking the object. The raw timings are at `~/.local/share/p5-desk-track-owner/scratch/ping-probe.out`. The same run showed that a client `close(1000)` on either socket ends as 1006 about 10 s later. The liveness check is therefore a protocol ping with no fallback, and the app's own close is a terminate (§G). (e) *§A's refused start.* An experiment on the Mac mini, to see what macOS does to a loaded agent that is turned off under Login Items, was withdrawn: the mini runs macOS 27 and the MacBook 26, and the design must not depend on either's answer. §A's four cases hold whichever step is refused, and `install-refused`'s words say "set up or start" and "load or start". M2 builds them. Case (iii) has one condition more than the architect's wording, that no live process holds `mawsd.lock`, so that a daemon which was already running and is silent stays `not-started`. (f) *M4.* 7e is worded as the acceptance is, with M2b's reading of "resumes" and the method of the file log and the two-path probe. Row 10's method is the one agreed with the desk track: a real model driven by a script through production's unit, not the replay harness. 11c's pass is restated under Decision Log 111, with staging's second member as its multi-member half. 14 is ordered: 14c first, then 14a and 14b on a kept session, with the pty host counted as the bound's suite counts it. 15 names the tailnet listener. 1d, 2c, 3b, 4q, 5a and 5b name their methods. (g) *Constraint 1* gains a sentence: M2b adds log lines to the engine host's stream and to the endpoint and changes nothing else of them. (h) *The verification*, as Decision Log 1 has it for M1 and M2: this amendment is reviewed for buildability by `doperpowers:adversarial-reviewer` before M2b's executor starts; the branch is reviewed at `doperpowers:reviewer-high` before it lands, for §F's table above all (that a link never runs two attempts, never upgrades twice for one probe's answer, and never waits in the window on a path that works); its gates are `--gates all`, `--gates packaged` and `e2e:remote-latency` on the head, the packaged run because main's file log is new behaviour in a packaged app. (i) *The floor*, the architect's change on reading the amendment. The first text gave a probe 1 s and left a path slower than that with no attempt until the window closed, which made a slow or lossy path worse than today for up to a minute. Now a probe has 3 s and thirty may be in flight for a target, and 10 s into a window with no channel a link takes up today's attempts again beside the probes, a target's answer still taking over at once (§F, the floor; §G, rule 3 for the feed and the project sockets; the taking over went with entry 8's rewrite). Two things were added in writing it: the floor counts from the link's first seek with no channel, whatever has answered by then, and a target takes over an attempt once in a window, so that a path which connects and never completes cannot keep today's attempt from the other carrier. A rule with no gate at all was weighed and lost (M2b's decision on the window). (j) *The hub's time on an upgrade, measured.* 2026-11-10, the desk track, on desk-hub-staging from the MacBook, six fresh connections each, timed from TLS done to the 101: the `?app=1` feed, median 252 ms (205 to 307); the Relay's app side, median 255 ms (208 to 282), against a retired machine name, so a 404 after the Access check, the registry resolve and the name's lookup, to which a live machine's 101 adds the Relay object's accept, a write and a presence read; `GET /api/me`, the same check and resolve with no upgrade, 190 ms to its headers; the network round trip to the hub about 15 to 35 ms. The hub's own time for an upgrade is therefore about 220 to 240 ms. The first text allowed 150 ms, unmeasured; §F now allows 300 ms, and the relay's limit is `7 h + 3 m` at most 500 ms. The raw numbers are at `~/.local/share/p5-desk-track-owner/scratch/upgrade-probe.out`. About 190 ms of it is the Access check and the Registry round trip that every request pays: a tracker row for the desk track (M5). (k) *A stated risk: the hub's probes.* While the hub is unreachable in a window, a bare TCP connect goes to its edge every 100 ms from this Mac, and the one that arrives is closed before TLS. Rules at the edge are not visible from the repository, and one that counts or drops such connects would make the probes fail on a path that works. What would show it: the hub's probes failing (`[reach] … none connected`) while an HTTPS request from the same Mac to the hub succeeds, which 7e's probe makes beside them (§K). The fallback is in the design: with no probe answering, the floor brings today's attempts and opens back after 10 s, and the window's close leaves today's backoff. The interval stays 100 ms. (l) *The feed's time is held in the gate.* §I holds the feed's `connected` and its last listings page to 2 s from the restore, on loopback, and does not only record them: without a bound the defect M2b removes, a feed that stays dead after a wake, could return with the gate green. The project socket stays with its unit tests and 7e, since the scene's development session composes no project channels. (m) *Relay to direct inside the window* stays as written, for the reason now in M2b's decision: a swap in the first seconds after a wake ends fewer byte streams than the 60 s recheck would end a minute into use. (n) *The owner label's worth.* sminos writes the alias as its writer wrote it, so an alias can imitate the label. The desk track will have sminos write the alias as a JSON string literal where it could be mistaken, after sminos's M3. §J's parse reads both forms now and says that MAWS's drawing is a display of the frame, not a verification; M5 writes the tracker row.
7. **2026-11-10, the executor, at M1's task review: what M1 deleted, ported and re-expressed**. The report lists every deleted case with what it pinned and where it is still covered. (a) *Deleted, no longer existing:* E11's live keep-quit probe; supervisor (a)'s, (h)'s and the packaged case's embedded forms (an unbusy quit stopping the supervisor, the lost-shell line after a keep-quit); daemon-idle's detached baseline and A-B-A legs with their helpers; c12-quit's dead embedded branch. (b) *Ported to `mawsd`, by the architect's ruling that they are shared behaviour:* supervisor (c), a kill -9 of an engine host holding a session nothing can adopt (the interrupted toast, the crash row, Diagnostics' crash line), and (e)'s relaunch reading of an idle release; the packaged case's fuse, `--detached`, process-group and SIGUSR1 checks; the zod-locale case, shape-independent, un-skipped. (c) *Cross-build:* the normal (update) and (downgrade) run both builds on fixed daemon shapes; a peer whose manifest lacks `machine.connectors` cannot cross with this build's `mawsd` (D11) and skips both scenes saying so, never switching shape (the task review's one finding: a first draft let such a peer drop to its embedded shape). The pinned scenes keep the peer embedded. On a daemon attacher the keeper's private `mawsd` ends by the attacher's build check (`asking it to restart`), which is the line asserted. (d) *Cold connector delivery* is re-expressed, not deleted: no O4 case asserts cold user and app HTTP sources located and sent once each with no session, and `delivery.deliver` cannot stand in (its Notion host allowlist refuses a loopback fixture). The app quits, `mawsd` and its engine host outlive it, and a second client of `machine.sock` asks `connectors.directLocate`, `directSend` and `directTake` through `mawsd`. (e) *artifacts-perf* ends the profile's `mawsd` and hosts before each cold trial with the launcher's `cleanup()`. (f) *The idle bound's records* stay as recorded and name f221bd3c2, this branch's merge base, as the last tree that can re-derive them. (g) *main-wiring's session-service cases* moved to where the service is composed: `src/services/spine-root.test.ts` (additional directories; the captured environment's defaults, history and scm trust) and `src/main/sessions/panel-lifetime.test.ts` (an unstarted chat's panel). (h) The plan mirror's fixture was re-captured by running its planner over the new `package.json`, not hand-edited.
8. **2026-11-10, the architect, at the adversarial review of M2b as written at d2353ab77: rework, three findings on the central guarantee and five more, all eight taken.** (Entry 7 is M1's record, written on branch `p5-o15`, and is left for it.) (1) *A TCP probe that answers could hold the working carrier behind a hung one.* One attempt at a time, with the other target remembered, let a hub whose TCP answers and whose upgrade hangs keep a working direct path waiting for 10 s deadlines, and stopped a failed target's probing through its backoff. Now the carriers are two lanes that race, each with its own probing and attempt under 2 s window deadlines, a lane backoff of 250 ms to 2 s during which probing goes on, six attempts a window, and on the direct lane a dial only while the bucket holds two tokens (§F). The floor stays and runs today's attempt whole beside the lanes, and nothing takes that attempt over: entry 6 (i)'s rule that a target's answer does so once in a window went with the rewrite. The sentence that said the race is never used inside the window is corrected. One departure from the ruling's words: when the relay completes first, the direct lane's attempt is not aborted but goes on and becomes today's switch, since aborting an attempt the bucket was charged for, to dial it again, would charge it twice. (2) *One attempt is not always one upgrade.* Catch-up redials stay exactly as they are; §F says an attempt is one `connectChannel`, counts every redial against the bucket's rule, threads the abort through each dial without touching the protocol package, and writes a line at each redial; the 1 s is claimed for the common case, in acceptance 6, the budget and 7e. (3) *The budget did not bound the endpoint's work before `subbed`.* That work is its own term, `J`. §I's case now has six subscriptions, of which two streams' cursors are really evicted by filling the production ring. Whether `J` is bounded by design waits for the architect's bench of the journal read; §F holds the marked paragraph and the candidates, and designs nothing in the engine host yet. No sentence presents the warm loopback figure as a bound for a journal read. (4) *The closing measurement could hide a miss.* The shell loops are replaced by one Node script of overlapping TCP connects, the app's own probe, with each try's start and end, the uncertainty stated, and `h`, `m` and `d` measured apart (§K). It stamps tries on the wall clock, not on one monotonic clock tied at the start, because that tie cannot be trusted across the sleep. (5) *The log's opening could throw before the repair window.* `install` never throws, makes and tightens its directory and file itself, and turns the file log off with one stderr line when it cannot (§H). (6) *Two instances could rotate the evidence away.* Only the profile lock's holder writes the file; earlier lines are held in memory and written with their own times; a second launch writes to its own streams only, and the first gains a line at the hand-off. (7) *The file is not content-free.* `main.log` is a private diagnostic that holds what main's console holds; only §H's lines are evidence, and they leave a machine through §K's patterns alone. (8) *Two startup tests were missing from "Touches".* `src/main/lifecycle.test.ts` and `src/main/sessions/main-wiring.test.ts` are named with their two edits and the merge rule with M1's. Beyond the findings, for the same reason as (1): an open of the feed or a project socket made inside the window is given 2 s, not 10 (§G); and the gate's stalled carrier is the direct one, on the relay's launch, because the hub's pipe also carries the feed whose bound the same case holds, the other direction being `link.test.ts`'s. *The measurement for `J`, the same day.* The architect's bench on master caaef871c (an M1 Max under a 1-minute load of about 21, warm file cache, rows of about 430 bytes shaped from the probe fixtures; `~/.local/share/maws-architect/o15-evidence/resume-cost/`): a cursor inside the ring opens in about 0.07 ms; an evicted one costs about 35 ms for each 10,000 journal rows before it, and the whole opening for a cursor just evicted took 16, 157, 670 and 1,714 ms on journals of 10,000, 50,000, 200,000 and 500,000 rows. The MacBook's seven lane B journals today are 34.8 MiB in all, the largest 23.5 MiB, about 55,000 rows. §F states `J` with these numbers and the journal size up to which each carrier fits, and says plainly that it is not bounded by design today. Both logs now carry it: the machine's line the milliseconds to `subbed`, the engine host's the rows read (§H). The design that bounds it is written as §L, checkpoints in the session stream: Task 8, built in M2b if the owner rules so, and otherwise the body of a board ticket; the ruling is recorded here when it is given. Lane A was read as well: its endpoint answers with no read, so its `J` is bounded, and after the hour its answer is always a snapshot whose content is sminos's to send (§F).
9. **2026-11-10, the architect, at round 2 of the adversarial review, on f0467597c: five of round 1's eight findings closed, three new on the lanes' composition with the floor and on what the common case left unsaid, two more; all five taken.** (Entry 7 is still left for M1's record.) (1) *The bound excluded neither a backoff nor a wait for the bucket.* The scheduling stays and the claim's conditions are completed as one list, stated in acceptance 6, in §F's budget and in M4's 7e row: no catch-up page; at most six subscriptions; round trips and `J` inside the arithmetic; on the adopted lane the first attempt after its first probe answer succeeds; and, for the direct lane, two tokens in the bucket. A wake outside them is neither a pass nor a fail: its lines show which condition it missed, the run is repeated once, and a second is put to the owner. The lane backoff now starts at 100 ms. The direct lane no longer waits for a refill: with fewer than two tokens and nothing in flight it is spent. One correction to the ruling's wording, from the bucket's arithmetic: two tokens are certain after a sleep of 24 s, not after any sleep of 10 s, since a token comes back every 12 s; the hour asleep is far inside it. (2) and (3) *Two diallers on one bucket, and no rule for a relay adopted by today's attempt while a lane was in its handshake.* The floor and the lanes are never concurrent on a link. A link is in one of three modes: *lanes* (a window, before the floor), *today* (no window), *today with probe triggers* (a window, after the floor). The floor ends both lanes for the rest of that opening of the window, and after it the link is today's loop with a target's answer acting as `probe()`; nothing is aborted there. So there is one direct dialler at a time and `viaDirect`'s check and charge are unchanged; §F's table is written for the three modes and for both orders in which two lane attempts complete; a lane's relay socket belongs to its attempt until adoption; the window's close lets a lane attempt in flight end before today's retry is scheduled; and a new opening of the window starts a link over in *lanes*. Entry 8's floor, today's attempt beside the lanes, is gone. Where the ruling's list named the window's close among the changes after which a completing attempt adopts nothing, the document follows the ruling's own rule for the close: an attempt in flight then runs to its end and is adopted, since the link stays in *lanes* until none is in flight. (4) *The probe's reader could count a try that spans the sleep.* `read` counts only tries begun after the wake's time, takes reachability as the start of the first counted try that succeeded, so that the try's duration is counted against the pass, and prints the time from its start and from its end (§K). (5) *A hand-written encoding of the normaliser's state is not lossless.* §L's checkpoint holds `v8.serialize` of the state, and the state was read for anything the format refuses: there is nothing. Also: the link writes a line at a subscription's first event or snapshot after its `subbed`, so the record shows when content resumed, for lane A's snapshot as for lane B's replay; "Does not touch" names the three files it shares with M1 and M2 as "Touches" does; Task 8 stays conditional on the owner's ruling.
10. **2026-11-10, the architect, on reading b6ac9460e before the review's third round: the floor aborts no lane attempt in flight, and it does not end the lanes for a path that has not yet returned.** Two defects of entry 9's floor, found by the architect's own read. (1) The floor aborted the lanes' attempts at its tenth second. A path that first answered in that second had its first attempt cut and was handed to today's attempt from its start; over the relay that cannot make the bound, and every one of the five conditions held. Now the floor's time starts no new lane attempt and aborts none: one in flight runs to its end, as at the window's close, and today's loop starts when none is in flight (§F, row 8). (2) The lanes served only a network that returned within 10 s of the wake, though M2b's own text promises the bound inside the window and denies it only outside. After the floor a target's answer started today's attempt, and `connect` gives the direct race up to its 1 s before it opens the relay. Now the link keeps waiting on each target that has not answered since the window's last opening, and the first that answers a link with no channel aborts today's attempt and returns the link to *lanes*, at most once for each target (§F, row 12); §G's rule 3 already did the same for the feed's floor open. The lanes and today's loop are still never concurrent, which was entry 9's ruling, and the three modes stand. The fifth condition now reads the bucket when the lane made its attempt, since today's loop may have dialled between the opening and the return. Rejected: a sixth condition, that the path return within 10 s of the wake, which matched the fifth text and left acceptance 7's sentence unmet on a slow network over the relay; a floor counted only from the first answer, which leaves a link with no attempt for the whole window if the probes cannot see a path a dial could use; and a gate case whose restore comes after the floor, because today's attempt can be mid-open at the instant of the restore and the case's count of one upgrade would then fail by chance, so row 12 is proved on the fake clock and read live in 7e.
11. **2026-11-10, the architect, at round 3 of the adversarial review, on 7d0307cb6, the last round on the specification: nothing against the three modes, the two orders in which lane attempts complete or their same-turn handling; two blockers taken and one clarification; from here the review is of the implementation, task by task.** (1) *The floor's time stopped the discovery of the other carrier while an attempt ran to its end.* Entry 10's first rule let a lane attempt in flight at the floor's time finish, and cancelled every wait meanwhile: a hub that returned a moment after the floor was not seen until a hanging direct attempt had run out its 2 s, and the relay's first attempt, begun only then, succeeded and was late with every condition holding. Now a lane that has had no answer since the window's last opening never stops waiting while the window is open, and its first answer puts the link in *lanes* afresh from the floor's time on, without touching the other lane's running attempt. The return is counted by lane and not by target, as the fourth condition is, so it happens at most twice in an opening and puts today's loop off by at most two more floors; a direct lane that may not dial, for want of two tokens, returns nothing (§F, rows 8 and 12). (2) *The subscriptions are written behind the acts a new channel resends.* `adopt` calls `acts.onCurrent` before `subs.onCurrent`, the channel writes in order, and an act still inside its 60 s can be as large as the channel's 4 MiB, so its transfer delays every `subbed` and none of the five conditions named it. A sixth does: at most 4 KiB written ahead of the `sub`s, acts and queries alike. The hour asleep meets it unless about a dozen acts are still open, since an act older than 60 s is only asked about; the link writes a line with the count and the bytes, and 7e reads it. The order itself is not changed: it is the link's behaviour outside any window, which M2b does not touch, and whether the `sub`s can go first is not known without a read of the order in which the endpoint handles what it receives; M5 files it as a tracker row. (3) *A link with a channel was an unstated exception to the mode table*, not a blocker. §F now says the modes are about seeking, that today's recheck is the one direct dialler on an established relay channel, and what happens when that channel is lost in a window while a recheck's dial is in flight. The reviewer's verdict on the round was to rework the two cases and then review the implementation, not the specification again. The architect took it and asked only whether the two counterexamples were closed (422e34863). The first is. The second was closed for one large act and not for many queries, which are written ahead of the `sub`s as well: the sixth condition's first text named acts only, and it is now on everything written ahead of the `sub`s, with the line counting acts and queries alike. The builder's task reviews carry §F from here.

28. **2026-11-10, M2b's executor, at Task 8 (c7a969f4e): what §L left open, and what it measured.** (a) `Checkpoint` is exported, since `session.ts` takes the type; the session has a `checkpointRows` option of its own for tests, 10,000 on both sides in production. (b) The journal writer shows its `position`, null while the writer redacts, since a redacted file no longer holds what the live normaliser saw. (c) The live checkpoint is taken right after `push`, before the warning rows are journaled, and only while the stream listens; the adoption fold takes none, and the adopted stream's first scan builds them. (d) A kept page cursor gives way to a checkpoint that stands past the rows it has read, so a forward jump does not read every row between; ordinary paging is unchanged. (e) A scan neither copies nor logs a checkpoint the keep rule would drop at once. (f) When a read from a checkpoint fails, the fallback's line counts the rows of both attempts and carries no suffix; only the newest checkpoint at or before the cursor is tried. (g) The live path's checkpoint and a scan's are byte-equal for every frame that survives JSON; a control request the host builds with an `undefined` member differs in bytes (the live state holds the member, the journal's does not) and gives the same answers, which the property test holds. (h) Measured on a bench journal of 200,000 rows, under a load of about 19: the live path's cost per boundary 0.11 ms for a state of 155 KB and 0.59 ms for one of 666 KB; a cursor in the dense region read 9,302 rows in 79 ms where today's scan read 199,302 in 1,408 ms. At most one interval of rows less one, plus the page, is read for a cursor in the dense region.
29. **2026-11-10, M2b's executor, at Task 8's review: two proof sentences of §L made exact.** (a) The live path's checkpoint and a scan's at the same row are byte-equal except where the live state holds an `undefined` member that the journal's JSON drops, as a control request the host builds with one does (Decision Log 28 (g)). The normaliser reads that request by property and not by its keys, so the answers are the same, but §L's proof said "byte for byte" without the exception, and no test held the answers for it. §L now states both: byte-equal where no such member is held, and where one is, a live-boundary test restores the live checkpoint and reads on, event for event equal to a full scan. A JSON round trip per row to make the buffers equal was not taken: it would cost the live path for a difference that changes no answer. (b) §L's "§I's case also asserts the rows read" is built as the bound on each resume read's rows, at most one interval plus the page. The review counted the scene: each stub turn writes about 313 rows, so the second launch's journal crosses 10,000 rows, but both held cursors are taken before that launch's 21 turns, near 6,500 rows, below the first boundary. The case therefore shows the bound holding and not the checkpoints in use; the property test and the dense-region row count prove those.
